Hele-Shaw flow with surface tension & kinetic undercooling as a sharp interface limit of a fully parabolic Patlak-Keller-Segel system with nonlinear diffusion Thanks: Supported in part by NSF grants DMS-2009236 and DMS-2307342.
Michael Rozowski

Abstract
. A large population limit of the parabolic-parabolic Patlak-Keller-Segel (PKS) system with degenerate, nonlinear diffusion, e.g., of porous medium-type − m m − 1 div ( ρ ∇ ρ m − 1 ) -\frac{m}{m-1}\mathrm{div}\,(\rho\nabla\rho^{m-1}) , is studied. We show, asymptotically, a sharp interface develops separating a region containing organisms arranged in a constant-in-time, uniform density from a region without organisms. Under an energy convergence hypothesis, we prove the emergent interface evolves according to a Hele-Shaw free boundary problem with surface tension and kinetic undercooling, and the free boundary satisfies a contact angle-type condition with the fixed boundary. Further, we show that, for well-prepared initial data, phase separation in these systems is, roughly, the result of some compatibility between an antiderivative for the population pressure and the convex conjugate of an antiderivative of the chemical destruction kinetics. When compatible, an energy for which the parabolic-parabolic PKS system is a gradient flow is a penalized Modica-Mortola functional.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Large population limits of Patlak-Keller-Segel systems

This work is concerned with the
parabolic-parabolic Patlak-Keller-Segel system

[
KS70
,
KS71
,
Pat53
]
(abbreviated parabolic-parabolic PKS system)
with degenerate, nonlinear diffusion and strictly increasing chemical destruction, the motivating examples of which are porous medium diffusion (
m>1
) and power law destruction (
q\geq 2
):

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\alpha\partial_{t}n-\Delta(n^{m})+\chi\mathrm{div}\left(n\nabla S\right)=0,\\
&\beta\partial_{t}S-\delta\Delta S=n-\mathsf{c}S^{q-1}.\end{aligned}\right.

In this model,
n
denotes the density of some microorganisms and
S
the concentration of some chemical signal produced by the organisms and towards which they are attracted (
\chi>0
, chemoattractant).
The parameters
\alpha,\beta\geq 0
are constants denoting the timescales for the relaxation of the organism density and chemoattractant concentration, respectively.
The chemotactic sensitivity
\chi>0
is a constant (in this work) that quantifies the strength of the chemoattraction;
\delta>0
is the diffusivity of the chemoattractant in the medium; and
\mathsf{c}>0
is the medium’s intrinsic rate of destruction of the chemoattractant.
The nonlinear diffusion term models the mutual repulsion of the organisms. By rewriting it as
-\Delta(n^{m})=-\frac{m}{m-1}\mathrm{div}\left(n\nabla n^{m-1}\right)
, the function
n\mapsto\frac{m}{m-1}n^{m-1}
is interpreted as a “population pressure” field among the organisms that can be attributed to their natural incompressibility. This nonlinear diffusion is in accordance with Darcy’s law: the organisms diffuse in a manner that decreases their local pressure.

The simultaneous, antagonistic effects of nonlinear diffusion due to the pressure
-\frac{m}{m-1}\mathrm{div}\left(n\nabla n^{m-1}\right)
and aggregation due to the attractive chemotaxis
\chi\mathrm{div}\left(n\nabla S\right)
are responsible for various lines of inquiry, especially in the case
q=2
. Of particular interest has been the possibility of finite time blow-up of
n
, which corresponds to the existence of a time
T^{\ast}>0
for which
\lim_{t\uparrow T^{\ast}}\left\|n(t,\cdot)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}=+\infty
.

In dimension
d
with
q=2
and
\alpha,\beta>0
, it is known that the existence of global-in-time weak solutions is guaranteed whenever
m>2-\frac{2}{d}
(see
[
Mim17
]
when
\phi
has homogeneous Dirichlet conditions for the parabolic-parabolic system). Otherwise, blow-up can occur for sufficiently large initial mass of organisms
\int n_{\mathrm{in}}\,dx
whenever
m\leq 2-\frac{2}{d}
(see
[
CS12
]
for
d\geq 3
with supercritical diffusion or
[
LM17
]
in a ball or the full space for
d=3,4
with critical diffusion). Similarly for the so-called
parabolic-elliptic PKS system
(
\alpha>0
,
\beta=0
), it is known
[
Sug06
,
BRB11
,
BR14
]
that blow-up may occur when
m<2-\frac{2}{d}
, while solutions are in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\Omega))
when
m>2-\frac{2}{d}
. In the
elliptic-parabolic PKS system
(
\alpha=0
,
\beta>0
), solutions are also in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\Omega))
whenever
m>2

[
MR24
]
, although the same critical exponent
m=2-\frac{2}{d}
is expected to apply in this situation.

Several recent works (see
[
KMW23
,
Mel24
,
MR24
]
or the survey
[
KMS24
]
) have shown with linear destruction (
q=2
) and sufficiently stiff pressure (
m>2
), so global-in-time weak solutions are known, that phase separation occurs in a singular limit corresponding formally to that of a large population observed at an appropriate scale of time and space. Phase separation in this context refers to the emergence of sharp interfaces that partition the organisms’ environment
\overline{\Omega}
into evolving regions
E(t)\subset\overline{\Omega}
of constant organism density
\rho_{+}>0
. That is, rescaled solutions of (
1.1
) converge to
\rho(t,x)=\rho_{+}\chi_{E(t)}(x)
. To understand the migration of the population, these recent works derive an equation governing the motion of the free boundary
\partial E(t)
that partitions the environment
\overline{\Omega}
.

To introduce the appropriate rescalings of (
1.1
) for the singular limits, one formally considers a family of solutions of (
1.1
) for which the total population of organisms is large. Since the population is conserved, this corresponds to large initial data

\int n_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}({\bar{x}})\,d\bar{x}=\varepsilon^{-d}\quad\text{with}\quad 0<\varepsilon\ll 1.

Then, one rescales the independent variables
(\overline{t},\overline{x})
to obtain zoomed-out variables
t\coloneq\varepsilon^{2}\overline{t}/\tau
and
x\coloneq\varepsilon\overline{x}
, where
\tau>0
is an adjustable time scale of an observer relative to the parabolic scaling
\tau=1
. The functions
\rho_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\coloneq n_{\varepsilon}(\bar{t},\bar{x})
and
\phi_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\coloneq S_{\varepsilon}(\bar{t},\bar{x})
then solve

(1.2)

\begin{cases}\alpha\tau^{-1}\partial_{t}\rho_{\varepsilon}-\Delta(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{m})+\chi\mathrm{div}\left(\rho_{\varepsilon}\nabla\phi_{\varepsilon}\right)=0&\text{ in }\left]0,+\infty\right[\times\Omega,\\
\beta\tau^{-1}\partial_{t}\phi_{\varepsilon}-\delta\Delta\phi_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-2}\left(\rho_{\varepsilon}-\mathsf{c}\phi_{\varepsilon}^{q-1}\right)&\text{ in }\left]0,+\infty\right[\times\Omega.\end{cases}

The equations are now set in a bounded domain
\Omega
with no-flux boundary conditions

(1.3)

(-\nabla(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{m})+\chi\rho_{\varepsilon}\nabla\phi_{\varepsilon})\cdot\vec{n}=0,\qquad\nabla\phi_{\varepsilon}\cdot\vec{n}=0\quad\mbox{on }\partial\Omega,

where
\vec{n}
is the outer unit normal to the fixed boundary
\partial\Omega
, and given initial conditions

\rho_{\varepsilon}(0,x)=\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(x),\qquad\phi_{\varepsilon}(0,x)=\phi_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(x)\qquad\mbox{ in }\Omega.

As a consequence of the rescaling, the size of the population is

\forall\,\varepsilon>0\qquad\int_{\Omega}\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(x)\,dx=1.

The formality of this rescaling is due to the fact that after rescaling space we ultimately set the equations in a fixed bounded domain
\Omega
independent of
\varepsilon
; otherwise, we must study a family of domains
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
. When the environment is dilation invariant, e.g., the full space
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}
or a wedge, this rescaling is not formal.

Depending on the time scale at which the system is observed
\tau_{\varepsilon}
and the relative time scales of relaxation
\alpha_{\varepsilon}/\beta_{\varepsilon}
, various dynamics governing the evolution of
\partial E(t)
have been derived in the large population limit
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
when
q=2
and
m>2
. For
\beta_{\varepsilon}=0
and
\tau_{\varepsilon}=\alpha_{\varepsilon}/\varepsilon
(the limiting case of
\alpha_{\varepsilon}/\beta_{\varepsilon}\gg\varepsilon
and
\tau_{\varepsilon}\sim\alpha_{\varepsilon}/\varepsilon
) the system (
1.2
) reduces to a
parabolic-elliptic PKS system
, and
[
Mel24
]
showed
[
Mel24
]
that the region
E(t)
containing the limiting distribution of organisms
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\rho_{\varepsilon}(t)
evolves according to a
Hele-Shaw free boundary problem with surface tension
:

(1.4)

\text{For all }t>0,\qquad\begin{cases}\Delta p(t,x)=0&\text{for all }x\in E(t),\\
\nabla p(t,x)\cdot\vec{n}(x)=0&\text{for all }x\in\partial\Omega,\\
\rho_{+}V(t,x)=-\nabla p(t,x)\cdot\vec{\nu}(t,x)&\text{for all }x\in\big(\partial E(t)\big)\cap\Omega,\\
p(t,x)=\rho_{+}\gamma_{0}\chi\kappa(t,x)&\text{for all }x\in\big(\partial E(t)\big)\cap\Omega,\\
\vec{\nu}(t,x)\cdot\vec{n}(x)=0&\text{for all }x\in\partial_{\partial\Omega}\big[\big(\partial E(t)\big)\cap\partial\Omega\big].\end{cases}

Above,
p
is a pressure
related
to a potential
\big(\frac{m}{m-1}\rho_{\varepsilon}^{m-1}-\chi\phi_{\varepsilon}\big)/\varepsilon
for the organism flux
-\rho_{\varepsilon}\nabla\big(\frac{m}{m-1}\rho_{\varepsilon}^{m-1}-\chi\phi_{\varepsilon}\big)/\varepsilon
(see (
2.23
));
V(t,x)
is the normal component of the velocity of the free boundary in the direction
\vec{\nu}(t,x)
of the outer unit normal at
x\in\partial E(t)
;
\kappa(t,x)
is the scalar mean-curvature (sum of principle curvatures) of the free boundary at
x\in\partial E(t)
(with the sign convention that
\kappa(t,x)>0
wherever
\partial E(t)
is locally convex);
\rho_{+}>0
is the asymptotic density of organisms; and
\gamma_{0}>0
is a constant describing the strength of surface tension. By
\partial_{\Sigma}(U\cap\Sigma)
, we mean the boundary of the set
U\cap\Sigma
relative to the subspace topology of
\Sigma
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The final equation is a contact angle condition: the free boundary must meet the fixed boundary orthogonally.

In an opposite regime
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=0
and
\tau_{\varepsilon}=\beta_{\varepsilon}
(the limiting case of
\alpha_{\varepsilon}/\beta_{\varepsilon}\ll\varepsilon
and
\tau_{\varepsilon}\sim\beta_{\varepsilon}
), the system reduces to an
elliptic-parabolic PKS system
, and Mellet and this author showed
[
MR24
]
that the region
E(t)
evolves by a
volume-preserving mean-curvature flow
:

(1.5)

\text{For all }t>0,\qquad\begin{cases}V(t,x)=-\kappa(t,x)+\Lambda(t)&\text{ for all }x\in\big(\partial E(t)\big)\cap\Omega,\\
\left|E(t)\right|=\left|E(0)\right|,\\
\vec{\nu}(t,x)\cdot\vec{n}(x)=0&\text{ for all }x\in\partial_{\partial\Omega}\big[\big(\partial E(t)\big)\cap\partial\Omega\big].\end{cases}

Above,
\Lambda(t)
is a Lagrange multiplier for the volume constraint
\left|E(t)\right|=\left|E(0)\right|
and is constant in space; it is the limit of a potential for the organism velocity.

This work examines an intermediate regime
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=\beta_{\varepsilon}\varepsilon
and
\tau_{\varepsilon}=\beta_{\varepsilon}
(the limiting case of
\alpha_{\varepsilon}/\beta_{\varepsilon}\sim\varepsilon
and
\tau_{\varepsilon}\sim\beta_{\varepsilon}
), and its main contribution rigorously establishes the result announced in
[
MR24
]
that, in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, solutions of (
1.2
) asymptotically satisfy a Hele-Shaw free boundary problem with surface tension and kinetic undercooling.

We set
\alpha_{\varepsilon}/\tau_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon\alpha_{0}
,
\tau_{\varepsilon}=\beta_{\varepsilon}/\beta_{0}
in (
1.2
), and we are led to the following system (which is a
parabolic-parabolic PKS system
with nonlinear diffusion):

(
P_{\varepsilon}
)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\alpha_{0}\partial_{t}\rho_{\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathrm{div}\left(\rho_{\varepsilon}\nabla f^{\prime}(\rho_{\varepsilon})\right)+\frac{\chi}{\varepsilon}\mathrm{div}\,(\rho_{\varepsilon}\nabla\phi_{\varepsilon})=0\quad&\text{in }\left]0,+\infty\right[\times\Omega,\\
\displaystyle\varepsilon\beta_{0}\partial_{t}\phi_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon\mathrm{div}\left({\mathsf{A}}(x)\nabla\phi_{\varepsilon}\right)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\rho_{\varepsilon}-\mathsf{c}(x)g^{\prime}(\phi_{\varepsilon})\right)&\text{in }\left]0,+\infty\right[\times\Omega,\\
\displaystyle(-\rho_{\varepsilon}\nabla f^{\prime}(\rho_{\varepsilon})+\rho_{\varepsilon}\nabla\phi_{\varepsilon})\cdot\vec{n}={\mathsf{A}}(x)\nabla\phi_{\varepsilon}\cdot\vec{n}=0&\text{on }\left]0,+\infty\right[\times\partial\Omega,\\
\displaystyle\rho_{\varepsilon}(0,\cdot)=\rho^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon},\qquad\phi_{\varepsilon}(0,\cdot)=\phi^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon}&\text{in }\Omega.\end{cases}

For the purpose of describing the intermediacy of this model’s sharp interface limit to the aforementioned models’ sharp interface limits, we have introduced timescales
\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}>0
independent of
\varepsilon
. We delay stating the precise assumptions on
f,{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{c},g
until Section
2
, but we point out that, as in earlier works on this sharp interface limit, we consider a class of pressure laws
f^{\prime}
including the power law we mentioned earlier. In contrast to prior works, we admit the possibility that the medium’s intrinsic destruction rate of the chemoattractant
\mathsf{c}
may vary over the environment
x\mapsto\mathsf{c}(x)
and that the destruction kinetics may be nonlinear
v\mapsto g^{\prime}(v)
. We will require that the diffusion coefficient
x\mapsto{\mathsf{A}}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be uniformly elliptic; only the diffusion of the organisms is degenerate.

One aim of this paper is to highlight that phase separation in PKS systems is a consequence of some compatibility between
f
and
g
. Previous works on these systems’ large population/sharp interface limits have considered, on the one hand, only the case of
\mathsf{c}(x)\equiv\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}>0
and
g(v)=v^{2}/2
. On the other hand, they allow rather general
f=f(u)
, provided

(1)

u\mapsto f(u)-u^{2}/(2\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}})
forms a double-well potential;

(2)

f
satisfies more than quadratic growth as
u\to+\infty
, i.e., there exists some
m>2
, and
K,R>0
such that
f(u)\geq Ku^{m}
whenever
u>R
.

In this work, we will show that phase separation in these large population limits occurs whenever
f
and
g
are compatible in the following rough sense:

(1
\circ
)

u\mapsto f(u)-\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}\,g^{\ast}(u/\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}})
forms a double-well potential, where
g^{\ast}
is the Legendre transform
1
1

1

The Legendre transform of a convex function
h\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{+\infty\right\}
is
h^{\ast}(u)\coloneq\sup_{v\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{uv-h(v)\right\}
.
of
g
;

(2
\circ
)

f
grows faster than
g^{\ast}
as
u\to+\infty
.

As a particular example, we show that if
2
2

2

We denote by
\iota_{C}
the indicator function of the set
C
, where
\iota_{C}(u)\coloneq 0
if
u\in C
and
\iota_{C}(u)\coloneq+\infty
if
u\not\in C
.

f(u)=u^{m}/(m-1)+\iota_{\left[0,+\infty\right[}(u)
with
m>1
and
g(v)=\left|v\right|^{q}/q
with
q\geq 2
, then phase separation occurs in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
provided
m>q^{\prime}\coloneq q/(q-1)
, which recovers the previously known condition of
m>2
in the case
g(v)=v^{2}/2
.

Another aim is to show that
\mathsf{c}
is responsible for a confinement effect in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
. More precisely, if
\mathsf{c}
satisfies some nondegeneracy conditions, the organisms asymptotically aggregate into a region
E(t)
satisfying
E(t)\subset\left\{\mathsf{c}={\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\right\}
for each
t
, where
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\coloneq\min_{x\in\overline{\Omega}}\mathsf{c}(x)>0
; that is, the organisms aggregate on the region in which the chemical towards which they are attracted decays the most slowly.

This paper’s main purpose is rigorously exhibit the convergence as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
of a solution of (

P
ε

) to a solution of the following
Hele-Shaw free boundary problem with surface tension and kinetic undercooling
:

(
P_{0}
)

\text{For all }t>0,\,\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta p(t,x)=0&&\text{for all }x\in E(t),\\
&\nabla p(t,x)\cdot\vec{n}_{0}(x)=0&&\text{for all }x\in\partial\Omega_{0},\\
&\rho_{+}V(t,x)=-\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}\nabla p(t,x)\cdot\vec{\nu}(t,x)&&\text{for all }x\in\big(\partial E(t)\big)\cap\Omega_{0},\\
&p(t,x)+\frac{\beta_{0}\gamma_{0}\chi}{\alpha_{0}}\nabla p(t,x)\cdot\vec{\nu}(t,x)=\rho_{+}\gamma_{0}\chi\kappa_{\mathsf{A}}(t,x)&&\text{for all }x\in\big(\partial E(t)\big)\cap\Omega_{0},\\
&\frac{{\mathsf{A}}(x)\vec{\nu}(t,x)}{\left|\vec{\nu}(t,x)\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}}\cdot\vec{n}(x)=0&&\text{for all }x\in\partial_{\partial\Omega}\big[\big(\partial E(t)\big)\cap\partial\Omega\big],\\
&\frac{{\mathsf{A}}(x)\vec{\nu}(t,x)}{\left|\vec{\nu}(t,x)\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}}\cdot\vec{n}_{0}(x)=\left|\vec{n}_{0}(x)\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}&&\text{for all }x\in\partial_{(\partial\Omega_{0})\cap\Omega}\big[\big(\partial E(t)\big)\cap\big(\partial\Omega_{0}\big)\cap\Omega\big].\end{aligned}\right.

For brevity, we have introduced the slow decay region
\Omega_{0}\coloneq\left\{\mathsf{c}=\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}\right\}
where the organisms are asymptotically concentrated, its interior
\accentset{\circ}{\Omega}_{0}
, and its outer unit normal vector
\vec{n}_{0}
. For each
x\in\overline{\Omega}
, the function
\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni p\mapsto\left|p\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}
is the norm induced by the uniformly elliptic matrix
{\mathsf{A}}
, i.e.,
\left|p\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}\coloneq\sqrt{p\cdot{\mathsf{A}}(x)p}
. We denote by
\kappa_{\mathsf{A}}(t,x)
the anisotropic mean-curvature of the free boundary at
x\in\partial E(t)
. If
{\mathsf{A}}\equiv I_{d}
, it coincides with the scalar mean-curvature and we use the same sign convention as in (
1.4
). Otherwise, we introduce the surface tension function
\sigma(x,p)\coloneq\left|p\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}
on
\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, and then

\kappa_{\mathsf{A}}(t,x)=\mathrm{div}_{\partial E(t)}\nabla_{p}\sigma(x,\vec{\nu}(t,x))+\nabla_{x}\sigma(x,\vec{\nu}(t,x))\cdot\vec{\nu}(t,x).

The fifth equation in (

P
0

) is the analogue of the contact angle condition from (
1.4
); the anisotropy and inhomogeneity of the diffusivity adjust it. It expresses the orthogonality between the fixed boundary’s normal
\vec{n}(x)
and the Cahn-Hoffman vector
\nabla_{p}\sigma(x,\vec{\nu}(t,x))={\mathsf{A}}(x)\vec{\nu}(t,x)/\left|\vec{\nu}(t,x)\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}
associated to the surface energy density
\sigma
and oriented by
\vec{\nu}
.

The sixth equation is a prescribed contact angle condition between the free boundary and the confining region’s boundary
\partial\Omega_{0}
in
\Omega
; it emerges from the confinement effect owed to
\mathsf{c}
. In fact, it reduces to a tangential contact angle condition:
\vec{\nu}(t,x)\cdot\vec{n}_{0}(x)=1
. To see this, multiply by
\left|\vec{\nu}(t,x)\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}
, which shows there is equality in the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for the inner product
(p,q)\mapsto q\cdot{\mathsf{A}}(x)p
induced by
{\mathsf{A}}(x)
, which means
{\mathsf{A}}^{1/2}(x){\nu}(t,x)
and
{\mathsf{A}}^{1/2}(x)\vec{n}_{0}(x)
are parallel. Since
{\mathsf{A}}^{1/2}(x)
is invertible,
\left|\vec{\nu}(t,x)\right|=\left|\vec{n}_{0}(x)\right|=1
, and each of
\vec{\nu}(t,x)
and
\vec{n}_{0}(x)
is outward directed, the only possibility is
\vec{\nu}(t,x)=\vec{n}_{0}(x)
. In contrast to where the free boundary meets
\partial\Omega
, neither the anisotropy nor the inhomogeneity of the diffusivity
{\mathsf{A}}(x)
affect the contact angle along
(\partial\Omega_{0})\cap\Omega
. We prefer to write the condition as in (

P
0

) as opposed to
\vec{\nu}(t,x)\cdot\vec{n}_{0}=0
to make clearer the connection between the contact angles that emerge and the limiting energy (
2.13
); see also (
2.17
). These last two equations in (

P
0

) are in agreement with Young’s law in an anisotropic setting; see
[
PM15
]
.

1.2.
Intermediacy of (

P
0

) to (
1.4
) and (
1.5
)

Suppose for a moment that
{\mathsf{A}}\equiv I_{d}
and
\mathsf{c}\equiv\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}>0
.

In the system (

P
ε

), neither the chemoattractant concentration nor the organism density is in a quasi-steady state, so this system is in a sense intermediate to both the classical parabolic-elliptic PKS model (
\alpha>0
,
\beta=0
,
q=2
in (
1.1
)) and the elliptic-parabolic PKS model (
\alpha=0
,
\beta>0
,
q=2
)
[
MR24
]
. We would like to describe how its sharp interface limit (

P
0

) is also, in a sense, intermediate to both of these systems’ sharp interface limits, (
1.4
) and (
1.5
), respectively.

Formally, in the limit of fast chemical relaxation
\beta_{0}\to 0^{+}
, (

P
0

) converges to (
1.4
), which is consistent with the formal convergence of the corresponding diffuse interface approximations, namely the parabolic-parabolic system (

P
ε

) to the parabolic-elliptic system (
\alpha_{0}>0
,
\beta_{0}=0
).

Further, one can formally recover a volume-preserving mean-curvature flow in the limit of fast organism relaxation
\alpha_{0}\to 0^{+}
, but not necessarily (
1.5
). In particular, we claim that the rescaled pressure
(\rho_{+}\gamma_{0}\chi)^{-1}p_{\alpha_{0}}
should converge to a Lagrange multiplier
\Lambda(t,x)
that preserves the volume of the individual connected components of
E(t)
. This is distinct from the Lagrange multiplier we derive in
[
MR24
]
that preserves the total volume
\left|E(t)\right|
but not necessarily that of different connected components (since
\Lambda
is independent of
x
). On the one hand, the fourth equation of (

P
0

) indicates that the normal derivative of
p_{\alpha_{0}}
along the free boundary vanishes as
\alpha_{0}\to 0^{+}
, and combining this with the fact that
p_{\alpha_{0}}(t)
is harmonic in
E(t)
implies that a limit
p_{0}(t)
should be constant on each connected component of
E(t)
. On the other hand, eliminating the normal derivative of
p_{\alpha_{0}}
from the fourth equation using the third equation shows all terms in the equality
\beta_{0}V_{\alpha_{0}}=-\kappa_{\alpha_{0}}+(\chi\gamma_{0}\rho_{+})^{-1}p_{\alpha_{0}}
are of the same order in
\alpha_{0}
, so this equality should pass to the limit
\alpha_{0}\to 0^{+}
, which would yield
\beta_{0}V_{0}=-\kappa_{0}+p_{0}
. This convergence would also be consistent with the formal convergence of the corresponding diffuse interface approximations as
\alpha_{0}\to 0^{+}
, i.e., of the parabolic-parabolic system to the elliptic-parabolic system (
\alpha_{0}=0
,
\beta_{0}>0
).

1.3.
Setting some constants to
1

While keeping the constants
\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\chi
in (

P
ε

) allows to see how they manifest in the limiting problem (

P
0

), it will be less cumbersome to work without them. We set

\displaystyle\tilde{t}\coloneq\frac{t}{\beta_{0}},\qquad\tilde{\rho}(\tilde{t},x)\coloneq\sqrt{\chi}\rho(\beta_{0}\tilde{t},x),\qquad\tilde{\phi}(\tilde{t},x)\coloneq\sqrt{\chi}\phi(\beta_{0}\tilde{t},x),

\displaystyle\tilde{f}(u)\coloneq f\big(\frac{u}{\sqrt{\chi}}\big),\qquad\tilde{g}(v)\coloneq g\big(\frac{v}{\sqrt{\chi}}\big),\qquad\tilde{\mathsf{c}}\coloneq\chi\mathsf{c},\qquad\tilde{\alpha}_{0}\coloneq\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\beta_{0}},

which produces the system

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{0}\partial_{\tilde{t}}\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathrm{div}\left(\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}\nabla\tilde{f}^{\prime}(\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon})\right)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathrm{div}\,(\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}\nabla\tilde{\phi}_{\varepsilon})=0,\\
\displaystyle\varepsilon\partial_{\tilde{t}}\tilde{\phi}_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon\mathrm{div}\left({\mathsf{A}}(x)\nabla\tilde{\phi}_{\varepsilon}\right)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}-\tilde{\mathsf{c}}(x)\tilde{g}^{\prime}(\tilde{\phi}_{\varepsilon})\right).\end{cases}

This is just (

P
ε

) with
\chi=\beta_{0}=1
, so we will state and prove our results for this case.

1.4.
Outline of the paper

In Section
2
, we state the assumptions on the functions
f,{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{c},g
appearing in (

P
ε

) and we give precise statements of our convergence results. Section
3
is dedicated to proving that a energy for which (

P
ε

) is a gradient flow
\Gamma
-converges to a weighted perimeter functional.

The remainder of the paper is dedicated to the proof of the convergence as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
of a weak solution to (

P
ε

) to a
BV
notion of solution for (

P
0

). Section
4.1
shows the standard result that, viewing the first equation of (

P
ε

) as a continuity equation with velocity
\vec{v}_{\varepsilon}
, a subsequence of solutions
\left\{(\rho_{\varepsilon},\vec{v}_{\varepsilon})\right\}
converges to a solution
(\rho,\vec{v})
of another continuity equation, which is the first part of our weak formulation of (

P
0

). Section
4.2
is devoted to the proof of phase separation of
(\rho_{\varepsilon},\phi_{\varepsilon})
. Then, Section
5
uses an energy convergence assumption to show the existence of a normal velocity
V
of the free boundary, and then Section
6.1
derives this and a distributional formulation of its anisotropic mean-curvature
\kappa_{\mathsf{A}}
from (

P
ε

). The former of these is shown to be equal to the normal component of
\vec{v}
in a weak sense, which is the second part of our weak formulation of (

P
0

). Finally, we derive the pressure
p
and the final part of our weak formulation of (

P
0

) in Section
6.2
.

1.5.
Acknowledgements

This paper was completed as part of the author’s dissertation advised by Antoine Mellet at the University of Maryland, College Park. The author would like to extend warm thanks to Antoine for his patience and generosity during the completion of this work.

2.
Main results

2.1.
Assumptions on coefficients and nonlinearities

For the diffusion coefficient
{\mathsf{A}}
, we suppose

(A1)

{\mathsf{A}}=({\mathsf{A}}^{i,j})_{i,j=1}^{d}
is a
(d\times d)
-symmetric matrix-valued function with each component
{\mathsf{A}}^{i,j}
in
C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
.

(A2)

There exists a constant
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\mathsf{A}}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{{\mathsf{A}}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{A}}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{{\mathsf{A}}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.33334pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{A}}$}\hskip-2.33334pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{{\mathsf{A}}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.66667pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{A}}$}\hskip-1.66667pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{{\mathsf{A}}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}>0
such that for all
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have
p\cdot{\mathsf{A}}(x)p\geq{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\mathsf{A}}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{{\mathsf{A}}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{A}}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{{\mathsf{A}}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.33334pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{A}}$}\hskip-2.33334pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{{\mathsf{A}}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.66667pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{A}}$}\hskip-1.66667pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{{\mathsf{A}}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\left|p\right|^{2}
.

As a consequence of
(A1)
, there exists a constant
{\overline{{\mathsf{A}}}}>0
such that
p\cdot{\mathsf{A}}(x)p\leq{\overline{{\mathsf{A}}}}\left|p\right|^{2}
for all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In view of
(A2)
,
{\mathsf{A}}(x)
has a unique symmetric, positive definite square root
{\mathsf{A}}^{1/2}(x)
, and

(2.1)

\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni(p,q)\mapsto q\cdot{\mathsf{A}}(x)p\in\mathbb{R},\qquad\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni p\mapsto\left|p\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}\coloneq\sqrt{p\cdot{\mathsf{A}}(x)p}=\left|{\mathsf{A}}^{1/2}(x)p\right|

are (resp.) an inner product and its induced norm.

For
\mathsf{c}
, we delay stating more precise technical assumptions until later.

(C1)

\mathsf{c}\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\coloneq\min_{x\in\overline{\Omega}}\mathsf{c}(x)>0
.

The following assumptions on the nonlinearities
f
and
g
are sufficient for the existence of the weak solutions of (

P
ε

) we use and ensure that
f
and
g
are compatible for phase separation.

(G1)

g\in C^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
is strictly convex and
g(0)=g^{\prime}(0)=0
.

(G2)

There exists
q>1
and
K_{1},R_{1}>0
such that
g(v)\geq K_{1}\left|v\right|^{q}
for all
\left|v\right|>R_{1}
.

Since only
g^{\prime}
enters the equation (

P
ε

), we can add a constant to
g
to reduce to the case
g(0)=0
, so this is no restriction. The condition
g^{\prime}(0)=0
is natural from a modeling point-of-view: the rate of chemoattractant destruction should vanish if none is present. Similarly, the strict convexity of
g
ensures
g^{\prime}
is increasing, so the rate of chemical destruction increases with concentration.
For
f
we require

(F1)

f\in C(\left[0,+\infty\right[)\cap C^{1}(\left]0,+\infty\right[)
is strictly convex, satisfies
f(0)=\displaystyle\lim_{u\to 0^{+}}f^{\prime}(u)=0
, and is extended to
\left]-\infty,0\right[
by
+\infty
.

(F2)

With
q
from
(G2)
, there exists
m>q^{\prime}\coloneq q/(q-1)
and
K_{2},R_{2}>0
such that
f(u)\geq K_{2}u^{m}
for all
u>R_{2}
.

In view of the mass constraint
\int_{\Omega}\rho\,dx=1
and that only
\nabla f^{\prime}(\rho)
enters the equation (

P
ε

), the function
f
is defined up to an affine function, so
f(0)=\lim_{u\to 0^{+}}f^{\prime}(u)=0
is no restriction.

We need to assume that
f
and
g
are compatible for phase separation to occur. This compatibility is described, in part, by the ordering of growth rates
m>q^{\prime}
from the coercivity conditions in
(G2)
,
(F2)
. We must further suppose

(F&G)

\displaystyle-\mathsf{a}\coloneq\min_{u\geq 0}\left\{\frac{f(u)-[{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast}(u)}{u}\right\}\in\left]-\infty,0\right[
, and there exists a unique minimizer
\rho_{+}\in\left]0,+\infty\right[
.

The number
-\mathsf{a}
is the slope of a supporting line for the graph of
f-[{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast}
that connects
(0,0)
to exactly one other point on the graph; see Figure
1(a)
. The existence of a minimizer in the definition of
-\mathsf{a}
is already implied by
(F1)
,
(F2)
,
(G1)
,
(G2)
.
The point is that there is a
unique, positive minimizer

\rho_{+}
with a
negative minimum
; that the minimum is nonpositive is implied by
(G1)
,
(F1)
. Given this compatibility condition
(F&G)
, the function

(2.2)

W\colon\mathbb{R}\to\left[0,+\infty\right[,\quad W(u)\coloneq f(u)-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g^{\ast}\big(\frac{u}{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}}\big)+\mathsf{a}u

is a nonnegative double-well potential with zeros at
\left\{0,\rho_{+}\right\}
; see Lemma
3.1
and Figure
1(b)
. If we omit the uniqueness of the minimizer
\rho_{+}
in
(F&G)
, it is possible to obtain multi-well potentials, which would result in multi-phase free boundary problems, the analysis of which is outside the scope of this paper.

The quintessential examples of nonlinearities that our results apply to are power laws:

(2.3)

f_{m}(u)\coloneq\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{u^{m}}{m-1}&\text{if }u\geq 0,\\
+\infty&\text{if }u<0\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{q}(v)\coloneq\frac{\left|v\right|^{q}}{q}\quad\text{with }m>q^{\prime}\coloneq\frac{q}{q-1}>1.

Since
g^{\prime}\in\Lip_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
by
(G1)
, we must impose
q\geq 2
. Then
(F&G)
is satisfied with

(2.4)

\begin{gathered}-\mathsf{a}=\frac{f(\rho_{+})-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g^{\ast}(\rho_{+}/{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}})}{\rho_{+}}=\frac{1}{m-1}\rho_{+}^{m-1}-\frac{1}{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}^{q^{\prime}-1}q^{\prime}}\rho_{+}^{q^{\prime}-1}<0,\quad\rho_{+}=\Big[\frac{1}{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}}\Big(1-\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q^{\prime}-1}}\Big]^{\frac{q^{\prime}-1}{m-q^{\prime}}}>0.\end{gathered}

Most of these assumptions we will make throughout the paper. For brevity, we christen them.

(SA)

The
standard assumptions
are satisfied when
{\mathsf{A}}
satisfies
(A1)
and
(A2)
;
\mathsf{c}
satisfies
(C1)
;
g
satisfies
(G1)
and
(G2)
; and
f
satisfies
(F1)
and
(F2)
.

The compatibility condition
(F&G)
is necessary for the construction of
W
(
2.2
). To make clear when this construction is important, we exclude
(F&G)
from
(SA)
and mention it explicitly.

2.2.
\Gamma
-convergence of the energy
\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}

The system (

P
ε

) has the structure of a gradient flow on the product
\mathbb{W}_{2}(\overline{\Omega})\times L^{2}(\Omega)
, so it is natural to study the convergence of its energy as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
. Any
(\rho_{\varepsilon},\phi_{\varepsilon})
satisfying a reasonable notion of solution for the system (

P
ε

) should dissipate the following energy:

(2.5)

\mathscr{E}_{\varepsilon}[\rho,\phi]\coloneq\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}f(\rho)-\rho\phi+\mathsf{c}(x)g(\phi)\,dx+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla\phi\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}^{2}\,dx;

namely,

\frac{d}{dt}\mathscr{E}_{\varepsilon}[\rho_{\varepsilon},\phi_{\varepsilon}]=-\int_{\Omega}\rho_{\varepsilon}\left|\nabla\frac{\big(f^{\prime}(\rho_{\varepsilon})-\phi_{\varepsilon}\big)}{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\varepsilon\big(\partial_{t}\phi_{\varepsilon}\big)^{2}\,dx\leq 0.

Essential to the singular limit of the evolution equation we study is the double-well potential property of the difference
u\mapsto f(u)-[{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast}(u)
. We thus add and subtract each of
[{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast}(\rho)
and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g(\phi)
to the first integrand of
\mathscr{E}_{\varepsilon}
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{\varepsilon}[\rho,\phi]=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\big(f(\rho)-[{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast}(\rho)\big)+\big([{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast}(\rho)-\rho\phi+{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g(\phi)\big)\,dx+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla\phi\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}^{2}\,dx+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}(\mathsf{c}(x)-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}})g(\phi)\,dx.

The second and third integrals are clearly nonnegative (recall
(A2)
,
(G1)
), and the second term in the first integrand is nonnegative by the Fenchel-Young inequality. However, even with this double-well potential property, the functional
\mathscr{E}_{\varepsilon}
is not bounded below for small
\varepsilon
, so we use the constant
\mathsf{a}
defined by the compatibility condition
(F&G)
and the mass constraint
\int_{\Omega}\rho\,dx=1
to introduce the constant

(2.6)

\mathscr{A}_{\varepsilon}\coloneq\frac{\mathsf{a}}{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\mathsf{a}\rho\,dx,

and we work instead with the augmented “centered” energy

(2.7)

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}[\rho,\phi]\coloneq\mathscr{E}_{\varepsilon}[\rho,\phi]+\mathscr{A}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\underbrace{\big(f(\rho)-[{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast}(\rho)+\mathsf{a}\rho\big)}_{\eqcolon W(\rho)}+\big([{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast}(\rho)-\rho\phi+{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g(\phi)\big)\,dx

\displaystyle+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla\phi\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}^{2}\,dx+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}(\mathsf{c}(x)-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}})g(\phi)\,dx.

The effect of this is the introduction of the nonnegative double-well potential
W
with zeros at
\left\{0,\rho_{+}\right\}
(recall (
2.2
) and see Figure
1
, Lemma
3.1
). Because we have only added a constant to the energy,
\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
is also dissipated by solutions of (

P
ε

). In view of Lemma
3.1
-
(i)
, the functionals
\left\{\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}\right\}_{\varepsilon>0}
are bounded below uniformly-in-
\varepsilon
(and in particular nonnegative), so studying their
\Gamma
-convergence is now worthwhile.

The term
\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{\Omega}[{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast}(\rho)-\rho\phi+{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g(\phi)\,dx
is a penalty enforcing that the range of
(\rho,\phi)
be near the graph of the subdifferential
\Graph(\partial[{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast})=\left\{(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\,:\,v\in\partial[{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast}(u)\right\}
that characterizes the equality case of the Fenchel-Young inequality
[{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast}(u)-uv+{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g(v)\geq 0
. Because the double-well potential

already enforces that the range of
\rho
be near the location of the wells
\left\{0,\rho_{+}\right\}
, this additional penalty enf

The next calculation, although elementary, permits rewriting the energy
\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
in a manner that presents it as a Modica-Mortola functional in the
\phi
variable with a penalty
\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{\Omega}f(\rho)-\rho(\phi-\mathsf{a})+f^{\ast}(\phi-\mathsf{a})\,dx
enforcing that the range of
(\phi,\rho)
be near the region
\Graph(\partial f^{\ast}(\cdot-\mathsf{a}))\coloneq\left\{(v,u)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\,:\,u\in\partial f^{\ast}(v-\mathsf{a})\right\}
. The following rewriting is at the heart of the emergence of mean-curvature in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
of (

P
ε

). By adding and subtracting
f^{\ast}(v-\mathsf{a})
:

\displaystyle W(u)+\big([{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast}(u)-uv+{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip
