2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g(v)-f^{\ast}(v-\mathsf{a})\big);

therefore,

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}[\rho,\phi]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g(\phi)-f^{\ast}(\phi-\mathsf{a})\,dx+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla\phi\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}(\mathsf{c}(x)-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}})g(\phi)\,dx

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}f(\rho)-\rho(\phi-\mathsf{a})+f^{\ast}(\phi-\mathsf{a})\,dx

(2.8)

\displaystyle\eqcolon\mathscr{F}_{\mathsf{c},\varepsilon}[\phi]+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}f(\rho)-\rho(\phi-\mathsf{a})+f^{\ast}(\phi-\mathsf{a})\,dx

(2.9)

\displaystyle\geq\mathscr{F}_{\mathsf{c},\varepsilon}[\phi].

(a)

When
(G2)
,
(F2)
,
(F&G)
are satisfied, there exists a supporting line
u\mapsto-\mathsf{a}u
intersecting the graph of the function
f-[{\mathsf{c}}\,g]^{\ast}
at
(0,0)
and precisely one other point.

(b)

The double-well potential
W
in
\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
is formed by subtracting the supporting line
u\mapsto-\mathsf{a}u
from
f-[\underline{\mathsf{c}}\,g]^{\ast}
. Implicitly,
W
contributes forward-backward diffusion to the evolution of
\rho
.

Figure 1
.
Double-well potentials constructed from differences of powers
f_{m}
and
g_{q}^{\ast}
; see (
2.3
).

We introduce the functions

(2.10)

\displaystyle{W_{\ast}}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\left[0,+\infty\right[,\qquad{W_{\ast}}(v)\coloneq\inf_{u\geq 0}\big\{W(u)+\big({\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g^{\ast}(u/{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}})-uv+{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g(v)\big)\big\}={\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g(v)-f^{\ast}(v-\mathsf{a}),

(2.11)

\displaystyle W_{\ast\mathsf{c}}\colon\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}\to\left[0,+\infty\right[,\qquad W_{\ast\mathsf{c}}(x,v)\coloneq W_{\ast}(v)+(\mathsf{c}(x)-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}})g(v),

which are essential for studying the limit
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
. The function
W_{\ast}
is a nonnegative double-well potential with wells
\left\{0,\phi_{+}\right\}
(see Lemma
3.2
), while
W_{\ast\mathsf{c}}
is an inhomogeneous perturbation of
{W_{\ast}}
(see Lemma
3.3
). We view
W_{\ast\mathsf{c}}
as a nonnegative potential with wells
\left\{0,v_{+}(x)\right\}
, one of which is space-dependent
v_{+}(x)=\phi_{+}\chi_{\left\{\mathsf{c}={\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.08888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.08888pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-0.77777pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-0.77777pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\right\}}(x)
.

Thus, the functionals
\mathscr{F}_{\mathsf{c},\varepsilon},\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}\colon H^{1}(\Omega)\to[0,+\infty]
defined by

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\mathsf{c},\varepsilon}[\phi]\coloneq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}W_{\ast\mathsf{c}}(x,\phi)\,dx+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla\phi\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}^{2}\,dx\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}W_{\ast}(\phi)\,dx+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla\phi\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}^{2}\,dx\eqcolon\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}[\phi]

are inhomogeneous, anisotropic Modica-Mortola functionals. Equality (
2.8
) shows when
(G2)
,
(F2)
,
(F&G)
are satisfied that
\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
, fundamental to PKS systems, is simply
\mathscr{F}_{\mathsf{c},\varepsilon}
with an additional penalty.

The identity (
2.8
) presents two viewpoints on phase separation for PKS. From the viewpoint of the definition (
2.7
) of
\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
, phase separation of the density is the result of a double-well potential
W
for
\rho
, and this induces the phase separation of the chemoattractant through the penalty
[{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast}(\rho)-\rho\phi+{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g(\phi)
coupling
(\rho,\phi)
. On the other hand, the identity (
2.8
) shows directly that
\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
is a Modica-Mortola functional with a double-well potential
W_{\ast}
for
\phi
and a penalty
f(\rho)-\rho(\phi-\mathsf{a})+f^{\ast}(\phi-\mathsf{a})
coupling
(\rho,\phi)
.
The emergence of these dual double-well potentials,
W
for
\rho
and
W_{\ast}
for
\phi
, is a consequence of the compatibility of
f
and
g
expressed by
(G2)
,
(F2)
,
(F&G)
.

As a final passing remark, this rewriting of
\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
as (
2.8
) is independent of the uniqueness of the minimizer
\rho_{+}
from
(F&G)
. If we do not impose uniqueness, then the dual potential
W_{\ast}
would have more than two wells, and
\mathscr{F}_{\mathsf{c},\varepsilon}
would be a multi-well Modica-Mortola functional.

The term
\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{\Omega}\big(\mathsf{c}(x)-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\big)g(\phi)\,dx
is a soft penalty on the support of
\phi
; it is responsible for the limits being concentrated on the level set
\left\{\mathsf{c}={\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\right\}
. For phase separation to occur, there must be sufficient volume in this level set to support a probability density with range
\left\{0,\rho_{+}\right\}
. If
\mathsf{c}
is constant, then there is no confinement effect, and there is sufficient volume whenever
\Omega
has sufficient volume. We assume:

(C1)

The region
\Omega_{0}\coloneq\left\{\mathsf{c}={\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\right\}
is a regular closed set, i.e.,
\Omega_{0}=\overline{{\accentset{\circ}{\Omega}}_{0}}
,
\rho_{+}\left|{\accentset{\circ}{\Omega}}_{0}\right|>1
, and
\partial\Omega_{0}\in C^{0,1}
.

The assumption that
\Omega_{0}
is a regular closed set implies each of its connected components has nonempty interior, so the limits are not a priori concentrated on lower-dimensional regions.

Theorem 2.1

(
\Gamma
-convergence of
\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
(
2.7
))
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
(
d\geq 2
) be a bounded domain with Lipschitz boundary, make the standard assumptions
(SA)
and suppose
(F&G)
,
(C1)
, and
(G2)
. As
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, the functionals
\left\{\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}\right\}

\Gamma
-converge w.r.t. strong
L^{1}(\Omega)^{2}
to
\mathscr{G}_{0}\colon L^{1}(\Omega)^{2}\to[0,+\infty]
defined by

(2.13)

\mathscr{G}_{0}[\rho,\phi]\coloneq\left\{\begin{aligned} &\gamma\int_{\Omega}\left|{\mathsf{A}}^{1/2}(x)\vec{\nu}(x)\right|d|\nabla\phi|(x)&&\begin{aligned} &\text{if }\phi\in BV(\Omega;\left\{0,\phi_{+}\right\}),\,\rho=\frac{\rho_{+}}{\phi_{+}}\phi\,\,\,\mathcal{L}^{d}\text{-a.e.,}\\
&\chi_{\overline{\Omega}\setminus\Omega_{0}}\phi=0\,\,\,\mathcal{L}^{d}\text{-a.e., }\text{and }\int_{\Omega}\rho\,dx=1,\end{aligned}\\
&+\infty&&\text{otherwise in }L^{1}(\Omega)^{2},\end{aligned}\right.

where
-\vec{\nu}
is the Radon-Nikodym derivative of
\nabla\phi
w.r.t. its total variation
\left|\nabla\phi\right|
,
\rho_{+}
is defined by
(F&G)
,
\phi_{+}\coloneq{[{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast}}^{\prime}(\rho_{+})
, and the constant
\gamma>0
is

(2.14)

\gamma\coloneq\frac{1}{\phi_{+}}\int_{0}^{\phi_{+}}\sqrt{2{W_{\ast}}(v)}\,dv.

More precisely,

(i)

(
\Gamma
-
\liminf
inequality) For any positive sequence
\left\{\varepsilon_{n}\right\}
converging to
0
, pair
(\rho,\phi)\in L^{1}(\Omega)^{2}
, and sequence of pairs
\left\{(\rho_{n},\phi_{n})\right\}\subset L^{1}(\Omega)^{2}
converging w.r.t.
L^{1}(\Omega)^{2}
to
(\rho,\phi)
, we have

(2.15)

\mathscr{G}_{0}[\rho,\phi]\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon_{n}}[\rho_{n},\phi_{n}].

(ii)

(
\Gamma
-
\limsup
inequality) If
g
satisfies
(G2)
, then for any positive sequence
\left\{\varepsilon_{n}\right\}
converging to
0
and pair
(\rho,\phi)\in L^{1}(\Omega)^{2}
, there exists a sequence of pairs
\left\{(\rho_{n},\phi_{n})\right\}\subset L^{1}(\Omega)^{2}
converging w.r.t.
L^{1}(\Omega)^{2}
to
(\rho,\phi)
such that

(2.16)

\mathscr{G}_{0}[\rho,\phi]\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon_{n}}[\rho_{n},\phi_{n}].

Since
\phi\in BV(\Omega;\left\{0,\phi_{+}\right\})
, we have
\phi=\phi_{+}\chi_{E}
for some set
E
of finite perimeter in
\Omega
. The object
\nabla\phi
is an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued Radon measure; it is absolutely continuous w.r.t. its total variation
\left|\nabla\phi\right|
with corresponding Radon-Nikodym derivative
-\vec{\nu}
equal to the measure-theoretic inner unit normal vector to the reduced boundary
\partial^{\ast}E
.
The fact that
\mathscr{G}_{0}
is lower semicontinuous for strong
L^{1}(\Omega)
-convergence can be seen as a consequence of the Reshetnyak lower semicontinuity theorem
[
AFP00
, Theorem 2.38]
.

For
(\rho,\phi)\in\Dom(\mathscr{G}_{0})
, we have the complementarity condition
\chi_{\overline{\Omega}\setminus\Omega_{0}}\phi=0

\mathcal{L}^{d}
-a.e. in
\Omega
, so up to an
\mathcal{L}^{d}
-null set
E\subset\Omega_{0}
. We see
\mathscr{G}_{0}
accounts for the weighted perimeter of
E
on
(\partial\Omega_{0})\cap\Omega
, but it ignores the part of
\partial^{\ast}E
on
\partial\Omega
, as in the classical perimeter functional. Indeed, for
(\rho,\phi)\in\Dom(\mathscr{G}_{0})
,

(2.17)

\mathscr{G}_{0}[\rho,\phi]=\gamma\int_{\accentset{\circ}{\Omega}_{0}}\left|\vec{\nu}(x)\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}\,d|\nabla\phi|(x)+\gamma\int_{(\partial\Omega_{0})\cap\Omega}\left|\vec{n}_{0}(x)\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}\,d|\nabla\phi|(x),

where
\vec{n}_{0}
is the outer unit normal vector to
\partial\Omega_{0}
, which coincides with
\vec{\nu}
on
(\partial^{\ast}E)\cap\partial\Omega_{0}
. The above formula highlights the differing treatment of
\partial\Omega
and
(\partial\Omega_{0})\cap\Omega
in
\mathscr{G}_{0}
. The second term behaves as a wetting energy in the classical theory of capillarity, with
x\mapsto\gamma\left|\vec{n}_{0}(x)\right|_{{\mathsf{A}}(x)}
playing the role of a relative adhesion coefficient between the free surface of the chemoattractant distribution and the boundary
(\partial\Omega_{0})\cap\Omega
. Formula (
2.17
) is one way to see why the contact angles on
\partial\Omega
and
(\partial\Omega_{0})\cap\Omega
differ in (

P
0

).

We make some comments about the proof of Theorem
2.1
. After establishing a compactness theorem for
\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
, the proof of the
\Gamma
-
\liminf
inequality is straightforward thanks to the lower bound
\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}[\rho,\phi]\geq\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}[\phi]
(recall (
2.9
), (
2.12
)), but additional effort is needed for the
\Gamma
-
\limsup
inequality. We first construct a recovery sequence for
\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\mathsf{c},\varepsilon}\right\}
, which also satisfies
\mathscr{F}_{\mathsf{c},\varepsilon}[\phi]\leq\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}[\rho,\phi]
. As in the proofs of
[
Mel24
, Theorem 2.1-(ii)]
and
[
MR24
, Theorem 2.3-(ii)]
, we can adapt a recovery sequence for
\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\mathsf{c},\varepsilon}\right\}
to construct one for
\left\{\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}\right\}
since the case of equality
\mathscr{F}_{\mathsf{c},\varepsilon}[\phi]=\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}[\rho,\phi]
is understood. Indeed, the identity (
2.8
) for
\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
shows, given
\phi
, equality is characterized by
\rho
for which the penalty
\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{\Omega}f(\rho)-\rho(\phi-\mathsf{a})+f^{\ast}(\phi-\mathsf{a})\,dx
vanishes. To obtain equality in the Fenchel-Young inequality,
\rho
must satisfy

(2.18)

\text{a.e.\ }\,x\in\Omega,\qquad\rho(x)\in\partial f^{\ast}(\phi(x)-\mathsf{a}).

Thus, given a recovery sequence
\left\{\phi_{n}\right\}
for
\mathscr{F}_{\mathsf{c},\varepsilon_{n}}
, we define
\rho_{n}
pointwise to satisfy (
2.18
) with
\phi_{n}
in place of
\phi
. Some care must be taken to ensure that a function
\rho_{n}
satisfying (
2.18
) can be constructed that satisfies the mass constraint
\int_{\Omega}\rho_{n}\,dx=1
. We also note, as in
[
MR24
]
, there is no mass constraint on the
\phi
variable for either
\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
or
\mathscr{F}_{\mathsf{c},\varepsilon}
; the constraint
\int_{\Omega}\phi\,dx=\phi_{+}/\rho_{+}
only arises in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
. That is, the mass constraint on
\rho
is retained and transferred to
\phi
. The emergence of this mass constraint is a consequence of the coupling between
\rho,\phi
through the penalties
\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{\Omega}[{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast}(\rho)-\rho\phi+g(\phi)\,dx
or
\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{\Omega}f(\rho)-\rho(\phi-\mathsf{a})+f^{\ast}(\phi-\mathsf{a})\,dx
and it is why the mean-curvature-driven flows are volume-preserving.

Several technical differences arise in the construction of a recovery sequence for the Modica-Mortola functionals
\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\mathsf{c},\varepsilon}\right\}
in comparison to classical results. First, if the wells
\left\{0,v_{+}(x)\right\}
of
W_{\ast\mathsf{c}}(x,\cdot)
varied through
\Omega
in a Lipschitz fashion and
W_{\mathsf{c}}(x,\cdot)
satisfied the appropriate growth conditions near its wells, then as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
the
\Gamma
-convergence w.r.t. strong
L^{1}(\Omega)
of
\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\mathsf{c},\varepsilon}\right\}
towards a weighted perimeter functional would be a special case of the results of
[
Bou90
]

[
Bou90
, Theorem 3.5]
or of
[
OS91
]

[
OS91
, Theorem]
. In our case, one of the wells is discontinuous,
v_{+}(x)=\phi_{+}\chi_{\Omega_{0}}(x)
. Despite this,
\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\mathsf{c},\varepsilon}\right\}
still
\Gamma
-converges toward a weighted perimeter. This a consequence of the domain of
\mathscr{G}_{0}
only containing functions supported up to the discontinuity set
\partial\left\{\mathsf{c}={\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\right\}
of the discontinuous well
v_{+}
, but not across it.

The construction of the recovery sequence for
\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\mathsf{c},\varepsilon}\right\}
relies on a construction of an optimal profile
\omega
connecting the locations of the wells of
W_{\ast\mathsf{c}}
in
\Omega_{0}
. To ensure the contribution to the energy far from the interface is negligible, the optimal profile
z\mapsto\omega(z)
should converge sufficiently fast as
\left|z\right|\to+\infty
, i.e., away from the interface. In classical results (with the sign convention that
\omega
is monotone decreasing), exponential convergence is obtained by requiring the double-well potential is no flatter than quadratic near its smaller well and no steeper than quadratic near its larger well, and the Cauchy problem for
\omega
admits a unique solution whenever the potential is no flatter than quadratic near both wells. For the examples we have in mind, the potential
W_{\ast\mathsf{c}}
is the difference between a power law and some translated and truncated power law (see Lemma
3.3
-
(iii)
), which may be flatter than quadratic near its wells. These considerations impose some technical assumptions on the growth of
g
near
0
. Namely,

(G2)

Let
R_{1},K_{1}
be from
(G2)
. There exists
q_{0}\geq 2
and
1+\frac{q_{0}}{2}\leq q_{1}\leq q_{0}
such that for all
\left|v\right|\leq\min\left\{1,1/R_{1}\right\}
it holds that
\left|v\right|^{q_{0}}/K_{1}\leq g(v)\leq K_{1}\left|v\right|^{q_{1}}
.

Near zero, if we quantify the flattness,
g\gtrsim|\cdot|^{q_{0}}
, then we can take
g
to be flatter than quadratic,
g\lesssim|\cdot|^{q_{1}}
,

2.3.
Weak solutions of (

P
ε

)

We make precise the notion of weak solution to the system (

P
ε

) that we use. Make the standard assumptions
(SA)
. A
weak solution
of (

P
ε

) is a triple
(\rho_{\varepsilon},\vec{v}_{\varepsilon},\phi_{\varepsilon})
for which

\displaystyle\rho_{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{m}(\Omega)),\qquad\vec{v}_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(\left]0,T\right[\times\Omega,\rho_{\varepsilon}dx\,dt;\mathbb{R}^{d}),

\displaystyle\phi_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{q}(\Omega)).

(1)

(continuity equation) The pair
(\rho_{\varepsilon},\vec{v}_{\varepsilon})
satisfies a continuity equation
\partial_{t}\rho_{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{div}\left(\rho_{\varepsilon}\vec{v}_{\varepsilon}\right)=0
on
\left]0,T\right[\times\Omega
with no-flux boundary conditions
\rho_{\varepsilon}\vec{v}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\vec{n}=0
on
\left]0,T\right[\times\partial\Omega
in the sense of distributions:

\int_{\Omega}\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(x)\zeta(0,x)\,dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\big(\partial_{t}\zeta(t,x)+\vec{v}_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\cdot\nabla\zeta(t,x)\big)\rho_{\varepsilon}(t,x)dx\,dt=0

for all test functions
\zeta\in C_{c}^{1}(\left[0,T\right[\times\overline{\Omega})
. The mass flux
\vec{j}_{\varepsilon}\coloneq\rho_{\varepsilon}\vec{v}_{\varepsilon}
satisfies

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\vec{j}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\xi\,dx\,dt=\frac{1}{\varepsilon\alpha_{0}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\big[\rho_{\varepsilon}f^{\prime}(\rho_{\varepsilon})-f(\rho_{\varepsilon})\big]\mathrm{div}\,\xi+\rho_{\varepsilon}\nabla\phi_{\varepsilon}\cdot\xi\,dx\,dt

for all test vector fields
\xi\in C^{1}_{c}(\left]0,T\right[\times\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\xi\cdot\vec{n}=0
on
[0,T]\times\partial\Omega
.

(2)

(parabolic equation) The equation
\displaystyle\varepsilon\partial_{t}\phi_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon\mathrm{div}\left({\mathsf{A}}\nabla\phi_{\varepsilon}\right)=\varepsilon^{-1}(\rho_{\varepsilon}-\mathsf{c}g^{\prime}(\phi_{\varepsilon}))
is satisfied a.e. in
\left]0,T\right[\times\Omega
; the boundary condition
{\mathsf{A}}\nabla\phi_{\varepsilon}\cdot\vec{n}=0
on
\left]0,T\right[\times\partial\Omega
is satisfied in the sense of normal trace; and the initial condition
\phi_{\varepsilon}(0,\cdot)=\phi_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}
is satisfied a.e. in
\Omega
.

(3)

(energy dissipation inequality) The triple
(\rho_{\varepsilon},\vec{v}_{\varepsilon},\phi_{\varepsilon})
satisfies, for each
t\in[0,T]
,

(2.19)

\begin{gathered}\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}[\rho_{\varepsilon}(t),\phi_{\varepsilon}(t)]+\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{D}_{\varepsilon}(s)\,ds\leq\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}[\rho^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon},\phi^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon}],\quad\text{where }\mathscr{D}_{\varepsilon}(t)\coloneq\int_{\Omega}\rho_{\varepsilon}(t)\left|\vec{v}_{\varepsilon}(t)\right|^{2}+\varepsilon\big(\partial_{t}\phi_{\varepsilon}(t)\big)^{2}\,dx.\end{gathered}

We recall the functional
\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
, defined by (
2.7
), is the “centered” version of the functional
\mathscr{E}_{\varepsilon}
for which (

P
ε

) is more obviously a gradient flow on
\mathbb{W}_{2}(\overline{\Omega})\times L^{2}(\Omega)
.

2.4.
Singular limit of (

P
ε

) and derivation of (

P
0

), Hele-Shaw flow with surface tension and kinetic undercooling

We make assumptions that are standard when deriving
BV
solutions of perimeter-dissipated free boundary problems. We name them to make their use more apparent.

(WP)

Given a positive sequence
\left\{\varepsilon_{n}\right\}
converging to zero, we say a sequence
\left\{(\mu_{\varepsilon_{n}},\varphi_{\varepsilon_{n}})\right\}
of pairs of functions on
\overline{\Omega}
is
well-prepared
provided
\displaystyle\overline{\mathscr{G}}\coloneq\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon_{n}}[\mu_{\varepsilon_{n}},\varphi_{\varepsilon_{n}}]<+\infty
.

(EC)

Given a positive sequence
\left\{\varepsilon_{n}\right\}
converging to zero, we say a pair of functions
(\mu_{0},\varphi_{0})
on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
and a sequence
\left\{(\mu_{\varepsilon_{n}},\varphi_{\varepsilon_{n}})\right\}
of pairs of functions on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
satisfy the
energy convergence condition
provided

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon_{n}}[\mu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(t),\varphi_{\varepsilon_{n}}(t)]\,dt=\int_{0}^{T}\mathscr{G}_{0}[\mu_{0}(t),\varphi_{0}(t)]\,dt.

If
\mathsf{c}
is not constant, then a confinement effect emerges in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, and the limiting free boundary problem is posed on
\Omega_{0}\coloneq\left\{\mathsf{c}={\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\right\}
, and we require two further assumptions on
\mathsf{c}
and one on
g
. The first is a nondegeneracy condition on the growth rates of
\mathsf{c}
and
\nabla\mathsf{c}
near
\partial\Omega_{0}
in
\Omega
:

(C1)

Suppose
\partial\Omega_{0}\in C^{3,s}
for some
0<s<1
and let
\vec{n}_{0}
be the outward unit normal vector to
\partial\Omega_{0}
. There exists a neighborhood
U
of
\partial\Omega_{0}
and a constant
K_{4}>0
such that for every
\xi\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\xi\cdot\vec{n}_{0}=0
on
\partial\Omega_{0}
it holds that
\left|\nabla\mathsf{c}(x)\cdot\xi(x)\right|\leq K_{4}(\mathsf{c}(x)-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}})\left\|\xi\right\|_{C^{1}(\overline{U})^{d}}
for all
x\in U
. Further, there exists a constant
K_{5}>0
and neighborhood
U^{\prime}
of
\partial\Omega_{0}
such that
\mathsf{c}(x)-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\geq K_{5}\dist(x,\partial\Omega_{0})^{2}
for all
x\in U^{\prime}
.

The gradient estimate is used in the convergence of (

P
ε

) to (

0

) to tame a term contributed to the first variation of the energy by the support penalty
\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{\Omega}(\mathsf{c}-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}})g(\phi_{\varepsilon})\,dx

Secondly, we require that

(C1)

(\partial\Omega_{0})\cap\partial\Omega=\emptyset
.

This assumption is not solely technical. If
(\partial\Omega_{0})\cap\partial\Omega\neq\emptyset
, contact angle hysteresis or pinning may arise at the “corner”
C=\partial_{\partial\Omega}\big[\big(\partial\Omega_{0}\big)\cap\big(\partial\Omega\big)\big]

in (

0

\overline{(\partial\Omega_{0})\cap\Omega}
meets
\partial\Omega

to

. In the case that

intersects

orthogonally, so if a triple junction enters

then the contact angle adjusts instantaneously. We thus only consider cases with no corners, one case of which is described by
(C1)
.

Figure 2

We also require a more precise description of the growth of

(G2)

Let

Theorem 2.2
.

Make the standard assumptions
(SA)
and suppose
f
and

are compatible for phase separation
(F&G)
. Given a positive sequence
\left\{\varepsilon_{n}\right\}
converging to
0
, let
\left\{(\rho_{\varepsilon_{n}},\vec{v}_{\varepsilon_{n}},\phi_{\varepsilon_{n}})\right\}
be weak solutions to (

P
ε

) with initial data
\left\{(\rho^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon_{n}},\phi^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon_{n}})\right\}
that is well-prepared, i.e., satisfies
(WP)
. Let
\vec{j}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\coloneq\rho_{\varepsilon_{n}}\vec{v}_{\varepsilon_{n}}
.

(i)

(phase separation) If
\mathsf{c}
satisfies
(C1)
, then there exists a (not relabeled) subsequence of
\left\{\varepsilon_{n}\right\}
as well as functions

(2.20)

\begin{gathered}\rho\in L^{\infty}(0,T;BV(\Omega;\left\{0,\rho_{+}\right\}))\cap BV(\left]0,T\right[\times\Omega)\cap C^{\frac{1}{2}}([0,T];L^{1}(\Omega))\\
\text{satisfying, for each }t\geq 0,\text{ }\rho(t)\chi_{\overline{\Omega}\setminus\Omega_{0}}=0\text{ a.e.\ in }\Omega,\\
\text{and }\vec{j}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{\frac{2m}{m+1}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\end{gathered}

such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\rho_{\varepsilon_{n}}=\rho
in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))
and
\vec{j}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\rightharpoonup\vec{j}
weakly in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{\frac{2m}{m+1}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Along this subsequence,
\lim_{n\to\infty}\phi_{\varepsilon_{n}}=\frac{\phi_{+}}{\rho_{+}}\rho
in
C([0,T];L^{1}(\Omega))
, where
\phi_{+}\coloneq{[{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,g]^{\ast}}^{\prime}(\rho_{+})
.

(ii)

(continuity equation) There exists a velocity field
\vec{v}\in L^{2}(\left]0,T\right[\times\Omega,d\rho dt;\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\vec{j}=\rho\vec{v}

dx\,dt

\forall\,\zeta\in C_{c}^{1}(\left[0,T\right[\times\overline{\Omega})\qquad\int_{\Omega}\rho(0,\cdot)\zeta(0,\cdot)\,dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\rho\partial_{t}\zeta\,dx\,dt+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\rho\vec{v}\cdot\nabla\zeta\,dx\,dt=0.

(conditional convergence of (

ε

.

Then along a further subsequence, there exists

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\rho\vec{v}\cdot\nabla\zeta\,dx\,dt=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}V\zeta\,d|\nabla\rho(t)|(x)\,dt\qquad\forall\zeta\in C^{1}_{c}(\left[0,T\right[\times\overline{\Omega}).

Consider the

Define for

Then, for any test vector field

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\rho\vec{v}\cdot\xi\,dx\,dt-\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}\int_{0}^{T}\left\langle p(t),\mathrm{div}\,\xi(t)\right\rangle\,dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{\gamma_{0}}{\alpha_{0}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}V\vec{\nu}\cdot\xi\,d|\nabla\rho(t)|(x)\,dt

\displaystyle-\frac{\gamma_{0}}{\alpha_{0}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\Big[\mathrm{div}\,\xi-\frac{{\mathsf{A}}\vec{\nu}}{\left|\vec{\nu}\right|_{\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\frac{\vec{\nu}}{\left|\vec{\nu}\right|_{\mathsf{A}}}:(\nabla\xi)^{\top}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\vec{\nu}}{\left|\vec{\nu}\right|_{\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\frac{\vec{\nu}}{\left|\vec{\nu}\right|_{\mathsf{A}}}:\big(\xi^{k}\partial_{k}{\mathsf{A}}\big)\Big]\left|\vec{\nu}\right|_{\mathsf{A}}\,d|\nabla\rho(t)|(x)\,dt,

where

is the duality pairing of

,

, and for a.e.

is the Radon-Nikodym derivative of

The approximate pressures

converge weakly-

to some

. The definition of the space

&\begin{aligned} &\left\{\zeta\in C^{1,s}(\overline{\Omega})\,:\,\text{for all }k\,\,\zeta|_{\partial\Omega_{0}^{k}}=0\text{ and }(\zeta)_{\Omega_{0}^{k}}=0\right\}\text{ if }\mathsf{c}\text{ satisfies \ref{hyp-c:grad-nondegeneracy}, \ref{hyp-c:interiorBoundary}, }\\
&\qquad g\text{ satisfies \ref{hyp-g:growthNear0}, and }f\text{ is defined by \eqref{eq:powerLawNonlinearities}.}\end{aligned}\end{aligned}\right.

The space of test vector fields

) is (with the same

&\qquad\text{ satisfies \ref{hyp-c:grad-nondegeneracy}, \ref{hyp-c:interiorBoundary}, }g\text{ satisfies \ref{hyp-g:growthNear0}, and }f\text{ is defined by \eqref{eq:powerLawNonlinearities}}.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}\right.

We emphasize in the case of
\mathsf{c}
not a positive constant that the derivation of (
2.24
) is only carried out for the particular case of porous medium diffusion, namely
f(u)=u^{m}/(m-1)+\iota_{\left[0,+\infty\right[}(u)
with

(recall

,
(G2)
). While we anticipate that (
2.24
) arises for more general nonlinearities, for instance,
f
that are comparable to porous medium diffusion for both small and large

, we do not pursue this analysis.

We stress that it is the triple (
2.21
), (
2.22
), (
2.24
) that is our weak formulation of (

P
0

). We briefly sketch why a smooth solution of this triple solves (

P
0

). On the one hand, the continuity equation (
2.21
) encodes the incompressibility of the velocity field
\vec{v}(t)
on
E(t)
. On the other hand, (
2.24
) encodes Darcy’s law
\rho_{+}\vec{v}(t)=-\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}\nabla p(t)
on
E(t)
, so the combination of these equations implies the pressure
p
is harmonic in the region
E(t)
containing the organisms (i.e., the first equation in (

P
0

)). The equality (
2.22
) shows
V(t)
is equal, in a weak sense, to the normal component of the velocity field
\vec{v}(t)
along the free boundary
\partial E(t)
, which, in view of the equality
\rho_{+}\vec{v}=-\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}\nabla p
, establishes the velocity law of the free boundary (the third equation in (

P
0

)).

A detailed argument that recovers the fourth and fifth equations of (

P
0

) is carried out by
[
Kra11
]

[
Kra11
, Theorem 3.1]
for an analogous weak formulation of the Stefan problem with anisotropic and inhomogeneous surface tension (Gibbs-Thomson effect). The strategy is to test (
2.24
) with vector fields normal to the free boundary in
\Omega
and supported away from
\partial\Omega
(and hence away from triple junctions). Derivatives on
\xi
are removed by the divergence theorem on hypersurfaces, which allows to derive the fourth equation of (

P
0

). The fifth equation of (

P
0

) arises from testing (
2.24
) with vector fields normal to
\partial\Omega
. The boundary terms that arise from the earlier application of the divergence on hypersurfaces necessarily vanish, which implies the orthogonality condition. Deriving the tangential condition is a bit more involved but is otherwise similar.

2.5.
Relationship to prior work

[
Cag89
]

[
Cag89
]
derived the Hele-Shaw free boundary problem with surface tension and kinetic undercooling (

P
0

) (in addition to Stefan problems with such effects) using matched asymptotic expansions from solutions
(u_{\varepsilon},\phi_{\varepsilon})
of phase-field equations. In particular,

\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{\ell}{2}\partial_{t}\phi_{\varepsilon}=K_{\varepsilon}\Delta u_{\varepsilon},\quad\alpha\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{t}\phi_{\varepsilon}=\Delta\phi_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{a}{W_{\ast}}(\phi_{\varepsilon})+2u_{\varepsilon},\quad\ell_{\varepsilon},K_{\varepsilon}\sim\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}},\,a\sim\varepsilon^{2}\text{ with }0<\varepsilon\ll 1.

where its solutions
(u_{\varepsilon},\phi_{\varepsilon})
are, resp., the temperature of a substance and a diffuse phase field describing the state of phase. The function
{W_{\ast}}
is a double-well potential with wells of equal depth located at the values of pure phase. The parameters
\ell_{\varepsilon}
,
K_{\varepsilon}
are related to the latent heat of phase transition and thermal diffusivity of the material;
\alpha
is related to a microscopic relaxation time;
\varepsilon
is related to the thickness of the interface.

With regard to rigorous sharp interface limits,
[
Son95
]

[
Son95
]
showed, unconditionally, that smooth solutions of the phase field equations

\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}+(1-\phi_{\varepsilon}^{2})\partial_{t}\phi_{\varepsilon}=\Delta u_{\varepsilon},\qquad\varepsilon\partial_{t}\phi_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon\Delta\phi_{\varepsilon}=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}{W_{\ast}}^{\prime}(\phi_{\varepsilon})+(1-\phi^{2}_{\varepsilon})u_{\varepsilon},

converge as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
to an integral varifold solution of the two-phase Hele-Shaw problem with surface tension (so-called Mullins-Sekerka flow) with kinetic undercooling. Then,
[
CC98
]

[
CC98
]
showed uniform convergence of classical solutions of another set of phase field equations to a classical solution of (

P
0

).
These results are thus only valid until the formation of the first geometric singularity.

Short time existence and uniqueness of classical solutions to Mullins-Sekerka with kinetic undercooling was proven by
[
Yu96
]

[
Yu96
]
. For the corresponding Stefan problem,
[
CR92
]

[
CR92
]
proved the local existence and uniqueness of solutions.
[
Yi97
]

[
Yi97
]
used this local existence result to establish local existence of classical solutions for (

P
0

) (with a Dirichlet condition on the fixed boundary) as a limit w.r.t. vanishing specific heat
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
of solutions to the corresponding Stefan problem, i.e., where the first equation
\Delta p=0
in (

P
0

) is replaced by
\varepsilon\partial_{t}p_{\varepsilon}-\Delta p_{\varepsilon}=0
.
[
GP05
]
studied
[
GP05
]
a variant of (

P
0

) where the surface tension coefficient varies over the interface
\partial E(t)
(e.g., due to surfactants); they prove short time existence and uniqueness of solutions in Sobolev spaces, improvement of regularity, and continuous dependence on initial data. Finally,
[
Muc07
]
in
[
Muc07
]
exhibited short time existence of unique solutions to (

P
0

) in anisotropic Besov spaces, studied the limit as the influence of the undercooling term vanishes (
\beta_{0}\to 0^{+}
in our model), and verified that the limit obtained satisfies (
1.4
).

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,\left|v\right|^{q_{0}}/K_{1}\leq{W_{\ast}}(v)\leq{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-2.22221pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-2.22221pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.55556pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 2.5pt\hskip-1.1111pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{c}$}\hskip-1.1111pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{-5.42776pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{\hphantom{\mathsf{c}}}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 2.5pt}}}\,K_{1}\left|v\right|^{q_{1}}
.

: We use
(v)
. Let

where we have used

and
